---
description: "How the ZX Spectrum Next adds its sound sources into one stereo sum: the five inputs and their bits, why the sum never goes below its silent level of 1,536, how much of the range real music uses, the 12 bits the audio jack takes and what happens past them, the one-bit converters behind the jack and the speaker, HDMI's 48,000 samples a second, and how Klive's mixer keeps the same proportions."
---

import { Callout } from 'nextra/components'

# ZX Spectrum Next: The Mixer and the Outputs

Every sound source on the ZX Spectrum Next adds its number to one sum, 28 million times a second.
Nothing is ever subtracted, so silence is not zero. And that one sum has to come out of a 3.5 mm jack, a
small speaker inside the case and an HDMI cable, all at the same time. How does a single number become
three different outputs, and how much room is there in it before the sound breaks?

This is the part of the sound system every program shares. A game's music, its effects, its sampled
speech and the beeper's clicks all meet here, and whether they come out clean depends on how they fit
together: a mix that is too loud does not merely get louder, it can break up. Knowing how the sum is built
tells a musician how loud each part may be, and tells a programmer why the speaker can click while the
headphones stay quiet.

This article is article 7 of the series; it follows
[The Big Picture](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/overview/), which introduces the four
kinds of source and the mixer, and it goes into each of them in full. The background page
[How a Computer Makes Sound](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/) explains the terms it
uses: a sample, a DAC, a filter, sound from a single bit and mixing. If you read only one article after
the overview, read this one.

### How this article is organised

- **The sum** ([Part 1](#part-1-the-sum)): the five inputs, the bits each one takes, and the sum they
  make.
- **Silence is 1,536** ([Part 2](#part-2-silence-is-1536)): why every input counts up from zero, why
  sources rest in the middle of their range, and where the constant level goes.
- **Headroom** ([Part 3](#part-3-headroom)): how much of the range real music uses, and what the
  audio jack does with a sum past 4,095. If you read only one part, read this one.
- **The audio jack** ([Part 4](#part-4-the-audio-jack)): how one pin of the FPGA makes 4,096 levels.
- **The internal speaker** ([Part 5](#part-5-the-internal-speaker)): the mono mix, and the beeper on its
  own.
- **HDMI audio** ([Part 6](#part-6-hdmi-audio)): the sum as numbers, 48,000 times a second.
- **How Klive does it** ([Part 7](#part-7-how-klive-does-it)): Klive's mixer beside the hardware's.

<Callout type="info">
Levels are in the **units of the Next's mixer**: the sum of one side runs from 0 to 8,191, and every
level here is a number on that scale (EAR is 512, one AY channel at full volume 255). Each side is mixed
on its own; the numbers are for one side. Figures of the hardware's sum and outputs are worked out from
the hardware's rules, since Klive does not emulate the outputs; the figure of Klive's mix is captured from
the emulated machine.
</Callout>

## Part 1: The sum

The mixer has two sides, and on each it adds **five inputs**. The beeper gives two of them, one per bit
of port `$FE`; each of the other three kinds of source gives one. The sum is taken again at every tick of
the **28 MHz master clock**, so a change in any input reaches the sum within a 28-millionth of a second.

The sum is a **13-bit** number, 0 to 8,191. Each input is a binary number too, and the mixer lines it up
at a fixed place in those 13 bits before adding. Where an input sits decides how much it can add:

{/* /fig:inputs */}
<figcaption>The five inputs as the bits of the sum they occupy. An input placed two bits up counts four times as much: that is how the DACs' side is multiplied by 4.</figcaption>
</figure>

- **EAR**, port `$FE` bit 4, is one bit placed at **bit 9 of the sum**: 512 while the port bit is 1, 0
  while it is 0.
- **MIC** is one bit at **bit 7**: 128 or 0. It is port `$FE` bit 3, combined with the tape input and the
  Pi's EAR bit (the [overview](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/next-audio/overview/#the-beeper-ear-and-mic)
  explains the combination). EAR and MIC count 4 to 1, as the 48K's resistors weighted them.
- **The AY chips** arrive as one 12-bit number per side, the channels of all three chips on that side
  already added together: **0 to 2,295**. One channel at full volume is 255. Article 4 explains how the
  channels are spread over the sides; with all three chips in mono, each chip gives both sides up to
  765, three times 255.
- **The DACs** arrive as one 9-bit number per side, the side's two 8-bit channels added (A + B on the
  left, C + D on the right), 0 to 510. Placed two bits up, it counts four times: **0 to 2,040**.
- **The Pi** arrives as a 10-bit sample per side, **0 to 1,023**, added as it is.

Every input at its largest gives 512 + 128 + 2,295 + 2,040 + 1,023 = **5,998**. The sum's 13 bits reach
8,191, so the sum itself can never overflow: the adder needs no check, and nothing is cut off.

The weights keep EAR and MIC at the 48K's 4 to 1, and make one DAC channel's full swing, 255 steps of
4, a little louder than all three channels of one AY chip together (1,020 against 765): room for drums
and speech over AY music, which is what the Spectrum's DAC sound cards were for.

## Part 2: Silence is 1,536

Every input is a count upwards from zero. None of them can be negative, and the mixer never subtracts. A
sound is a movement **up and down around some level**, and here that level is whatever the sources add
while they are quiet.

That level is not zero, because two of the sources **rest in the middle** of their range:

- A **DAC** channel rests at `$80`, 128, halfway up its 0–255 range. A sample is a movement around that
  middle, so it has room to swing both ways. The two channels of a side add 256, which counts four times:
  **1,024**.
- The **Pi** rests at 512, the middle of its 0–1,023, for the same reason.
- The **AY chips** and the **beeper** rest at 0: an AY channel at volume 0, and EAR and MIC low.

So a silent Next adds **1,536** on each side, and stays there however long nothing plays.

Resting in the middle matters most at the moment a source is switched on. If the DACs rested at 0 and
jumped to their middle when a program enabled them, the sum would leap by 1,024 in an instant, and the
speaker would answer with a loud **click**. The Next avoids that by holding them at `$80` **even while they
are switched off**: the Peripheral 3 Setting register, NextReg `$08`, bit 3 enables the DACs, and while it
is 0 every channel is held at `$80`, so switching them on or off changes nothing you can hear. The Pi does
the same: switched off or muted, it adds its resting 512, not 0. The beeper cannot do this: the first time
a program sets port `$FE` bit 4, the sum rises by 512, which is exactly the click a 48K made too.

The constant 1,536 is not lost on its way out, but it is never heard. After the converters, outside the
FPGA, the jack's circuit lets only changes through, as the background page describes for
[a capacitor](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/#several-sounds-at-once-are-added-together);
what reaches the headphones is the sound moving around zero.

## Part 3: Headroom

How much of the range do real mixes use? Here are six of them, as the Next's hardware adds them: on the
sum's 0 to 8,191 scale, with a line at 4,095, which matters for the jack. They are worked out from the
mixer's rules, not captured; Klive's own range, which is counted differently, is in
[Part 7](#part-7-how-klive-does-it).

A 128K tune on one chip at full volume reaches 2,046. TurboSound's nine channels at full volume reach 3,066, and with the beeper's EAR and MIC on top 3,706: all below the line. Three chips in mono at full volume with EAR reach 4,343, past the jack's limit. Every source at its largest reaches 5,998, also past it, though well inside the sum's own range." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

  <line x1="20" y1="310" x2="20" y2="318" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="20" y="332" textAnchor="start" fill="currentColor" fontSize="11">0</text>

  <text x="143.8" y="332" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="11">1,536</text>
  <line x1="350" y1="310" x2="350" y2="318" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="350" y="332" textAnchor="middle" fill="currentColor" fontSize="11">4,095</text>
  <line x1="680" y1="310" x2="680" y2="318" stroke="currentColor" strokeWidth="1" strokeOpacity="0.5" />
  <text x="680" y="332" textAnchor="end" fill="currentColor" fontSize="11">8,191</text>
</svg>
{/* /fig:headroom */}
<figcaption>Six mixes on the hardware's sum, drawn to scale. Grey is the silent level; the coloured parts are the sources on top of it. Past the red line, the audio jack can no longer play the sum as it is.</figcaption>
</figure>

Worked out, for the left side:

1. **Silence**: the DACs at rest, 1,024, and the Pi at rest, 512: **1,536**.
2. **A 128K tune on one chip**, all three channels at full volume: on the left, channels A and B arrive
   (article 4), 2 × 255 = 510: **2,046**.
3. **TurboSound**, nine channels at full volume: three chips' 510 each, 1,530: **3,066**.
4. **The same, with the beeper on top**, EAR and MIC both high, 640: **3,706**.
5. **Three chips in mono** at full volume, each giving 765 to both sides, 2,295, with EAR on top:
   **4,343**.
6. **Every source at its largest**, the DACs and the Pi at full scale instead of resting: **5,998**.

The sum handles all six: it has room up to 8,191. **The audio jack does not.** Its converters take only
the **lowest 12 bits** of the sum, 0 to 4,095, and leave bit 12 out. A sum below 4,096 arrives whole. A
sum of 4,096 or more loses its top bit, which takes 4,096 off it: 4,343 arrives as 247. The sound does
not stop at a ceiling, as the background page's
[clipping](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/#several-sounds-at-once-are-added-together)
would; it **wraps around** to the bottom of the range:

<figure>
{/* fig:wrap */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 280" role="img" aria-label="Two milliseconds of a loud 1,000 Hz tone whose sum swings from 1,300 to 4,700, drawn as a dashed curve, with a line at 4,095. The jack takes only the lowest 12 bits of the sum, so wherever the sum passes 4,095 the jack's value drops by 4,096 and starts again near zero: the solid curve follows the sum until it reaches the line, then falls to the bottom of the range, climbs, and jumps back up when the sum comes down again." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:wrap */}
<figcaption>A tone too loud for the jack. Each time the sum passes 4,095, the jack's value falls by 4,096, and the smooth wave becomes a series of jumps: a hard crackle, much harsher than a flattened top.</figcaption>
</figure>

In practice the jack has **2,559** units of room above silence, and plays everything a 128K tune or
TurboSound in stereo can produce, with the beeper on top. What goes past is the extreme: three chips in mono at full volume
with the beeper, or a DAC or Pi sample near full scale on top of loud music. A musician who keeps the
total below 4,096 has nothing to worry about; HDMI, which carries the whole 13 bits (Part 6), never wraps
at all.

## Part 4: The audio jack

The jack's sound leaves the FPGA as **one pin per side** that can only be on or off. Making 4,096
different levels from it is the job of a **one-bit converter**, the delta-sigma kind the background page
describes under
[sound from a single bit](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/#sound-from-a-single-bit).

It works on the 28 MHz clock, one decision per tick:

1. The converter keeps a running total. At every tick it adds the input, the 12-bit value from the sum.
2. If the total is in the top half of its own range, the pin is **on** for this tick, and 4,096 is taken
   off the total. Otherwise the pin is **off**.
3. Over many ticks, the pin is on for exactly the share of ticks that the input is of 4,096. An input of
   1,024 switches it on one tick in four; 1,536, three ticks in eight.

<figure>
{/* fig:converter */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 270" role="img" aria-label="The jack's one-bit converter, clock by clock, for three input values, 48 clocks of 28 MHz each. For 1,024 the output pin is on one clock in four; for 1,536, three clocks in eight; for 3,072, three clocks in four. The share of clocks the pin is on is the value divided by 4,096, and the resistor and capacitor after the pin turn that share into a voltage." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:converter */}
<figcaption>The jack's converter for three inputs, 48 ticks each, computed exactly as the converter works. The pattern of on and off ticks repeats, and its average, dashed, is the input divided by 4,096.</figcaption>
</figure>

The pin's pulses are far too fast to hear: the pattern repeats every few ticks, millions of times a
second. A **resistor and a capacitor** on the board after the pin, a low-pass filter, average them into a
smooth voltage: the larger the input, the higher the voltage. Then the circuit that removes the constant
level (Part 2) leaves the sound alone for the headphones or the amplifier.

Two things follow from this design. The jack has **no sample rate**: its converter follows the sum as it
changes, at 28 MHz, so nothing is ever sampled and nothing is aliased on the way out. And its resolution
is the whole 12 bits: a change of a single unit in the sum moves the share of on-ticks by one in 4,096.

## Part 5: The internal speaker

The Next has a small speaker of its own on the board, driven by one more one-bit converter of the same
kind. It has no left and right, so it gets a **mono mix**: the left and right values the
jack takes, both 12 bits, added together. Some boards bias the result to suit the transistor that drives
the speaker, but the idea is the same on all of them.

Two registers control it:

- The **Peripheral 3 Setting register, NextReg `$08`**, bit 4 switches the speaker **on** (1, the
  power-on value) or off. Off, the converter keeps running but its pin is held low.
- The **Peripheral 2 Setting register, NextReg `$06`**, bit 6 makes the speaker play **the beeper only**.
  With it set and the speaker on, EAR and MIC leave the mix of the jack and HDMI altogether, and the
  speaker gets a word of its own with nothing in it but them.

What each output takes from the sum, bit by bit:

<figcaption>How the outputs take the 13-bit sum. The jack drops bit 12; HDMI sends all 13 bits inside a 16-bit word; the speaker, when it plays only the beeper, gets EAR and MIC alone.</figcaption>
</figure>

In the beeper-only word, EAR is bit 10 and MIC bit 8: 1,024 and 256 of the speaker's 8,191, the same
4-to-1 ratio, and as loud as EAR and MIC are in the speaker's normal mix, where each side brings its
512 and 128. That mode is for a program
that wants its key clicks and alerts to stay in the machine while its music goes to the headphones: the
beeper's sound is moved, not copied, so the headphones no longer hear it.

## Part 6: HDMI audio

HDMI carries the picture as numbers, and its sound as numbers too: **48,000 samples a second** for each
side, travelling between the lines of the picture. The Next builds each sample as a **16-bit word**: a 0,
the whole 13-bit sum, and two 0 bits at the bottom (the figure in Part 5). The word is therefore the sum
times 4, and it never wraps: 5,998 arrives as 23,992.

The **Peripheral 4 Setting register, NextReg `$09`**, bit 2 silences HDMI audio, for a television whose
own speakers should stay quiet while the sound goes through the jack.

Unlike the jack, HDMI **samples** the sum. 48,000 times a second it takes the sum's value at that moment,
without averaging what happened since the last one. A change that falls between two samples is seen only
at the next one, up to a 48,000th of a second (21 µs) late:

The dots are the moments HDMI takes the sum's value, 48,000 times a second, and the thick staircase is what HDMI sends: each edge moves to the next dot, so the steps are three or four samples long, not the even three and a half of the real wave." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

{/* /fig:hdmi */}
<figcaption>A fast beeper tone and what HDMI makes of it, worked out from the sampling rule. Every edge moves to the next sample, so the steps come out three or four samples long instead of an even three and a half.</figcaption>
</figure>

For most sound this does not matter: 48,000 samples a second is enough for anything a human hears, as
the background page explains under
[Fast enough](/en/under-the-hood/emulator-internals/computer-sound/#fast-enough-or-it-sounds-wrong).
For the sharp edges of the beeper it is a small cost: an edge that moves by up to one sample adds a faint
roughness, a little aliasing, to a high beeper tone that the jack plays clean. Article 8 shows the same
choice in an emulator, which must sample too.

The constant level travels with the numbers: a silent Next sends 1,536 × 4 = 6,144 in every word.

## Part 7: How Klive does it

Klive does not emulate the outputs. It makes **one** stream of samples, at the rate the IDE's audio runs
at (48,000 a second by default), and hands it to the computer's sound card; that stream plays
the part of the jack.

### The same proportions, a different zero

Each time it needs a sample, Klive's mixer asks every source for its level at that moment and adds them
in the hardware's units, with three changes:

- The **DACs and the Pi** are counted from their resting levels: a DAC channel at `$80` and the Pi at 512
  add 0. Silence is 0 instead of 1,536.
- The **beeper's** EAR and MIC pass through a filter that removes their constant level slowly (a constant
  falls to about a third in a little over a tenth of a second), as the circuit after the jack's converters
  does. A beeper wave
  ends up moving around zero.
- The **AY chips** are added as they are, from 0 upwards.

The sum is then multiplied by **12.8** and becomes a signed 16-bit sample, −32,768 to 32,767. Here is the
beeper switched on from silence, as the hardware sums it and as Klive mixes it:

<figure>
{/* fig:klive */}
<svg viewBox="0 0 700 360" role="img" aria-label="The same scene twice over 0.4 seconds: the beeper switched on from silence, a square wave of 104 Hz. Above, the hardware's sum: the wave moves between 1,536 and 2,048, with silence at 1,536, all of it above zero. Below, Klive's mix, captured from the machine: the same 512-unit square, which starts with its top at 512 above zero and drifts down over about a tenth of a second until it is centred on zero, from +256 to −256, as the capacitor after a real converter would leave it." style={{ width: "100%", maxWidth: "700px", height: "auto" }}>

<figcaption>The beeper switched on from silence. Top, the hardware's sum, worked out from the rules: 1,536 and 2,048, all of it above zero. Bottom, Klive's mix, captured from the emulated machine: the same 512-unit square, sliding down to centre on zero within about a tenth of a second.</figcaption>
</figure>

You hear the same thing from both: the constant level and its slow drift are far below any pitch. A
waveform drawn from Klive's samples sits around zero, which is why this series draws its captures about
their middle level.

### Klive's headroom is the jack's

The factor 12.8 is chosen so that Klive's range matches the jack's. The jack plays the sum cleanly up to
4,095, which is 2,559 above the silent 1,536; Klive maps 2,559 units to 2,559 × 12.8 = 32,755, just
inside its largest sample. So **every mix the jack plays cleanly, Klive plays without loss**, nine AY
channels at full volume and the beeper included. A mix that passes 4,095 is cut off flat at Klive's limit
instead of wrapping around: it is broken either way, and a flat top is the gentler of the two.

Before this was settled, Klive multiplied by 29.44 and ran out of range at 1,113 units above silence, so
three AY chips at full volume (1,530 units) were cut off in Klive although the Next plays them cleanly.

### What Klive leaves out

- The **internal speaker** is not emulated, so NextReg `$08` bit 4 changes nothing you hear. With NextReg
  `$06` bit 6 also set, the beeper belongs to the speaker alone, and Klive, which has no speaker, does not
  play it.
- **HDMI audio** and its silence bit are not emulated; Klive's stream is the jack's.
- The **wrap** past 4,095 is not emulated: Klive clips instead (above).
- The **tape input** is not added to the MIC line.

### Where to read the code

| What | Where |
|---|---|
| The sum, the resting levels, the beeper's filter, the factor 12.8 and the limit | `src/emu/machines/zxNext/wasm/zxnext/zxnext-audio-mixer.c` |
| The beeper-only switch (NextReg `$06` bit 6 with `$08` bit 4) | the same file, where the sources are read |
| Each source's level | `zxnext-beeper.c`, `zxnext-psg.c`, `zxnext-dac.c`, `zxnext-pi-i2s.c` in the same folder |

## Summary

- On each side, the mixer adds **five inputs** at 28 MHz: EAR (512, bit 9), MIC (128, bit 7), the AY chips
  (0–2,295), the DACs (0–510 × 4 = 0–2,040) and the Pi (0–1,023). Every input at its largest gives
  **5,998**, inside the 13-bit sum's **8,191**, so the sum never overflows.
- Every input counts up from zero. The DACs rest at `$80` (1,024 per side) and the Pi at 512, even when
  switched off, so switching them never clicks: a silent Next sums to **1,536**. The circuit after the
  converters removes that constant level.
- The **audio jack** takes the sum's lowest **12 bits**: up to 4,095, 2,559 above silence. Nine AY
  channels at full volume and the beeper fit; past 4,095 the jack's value **wraps around**, a hard crackle.
- The jack's **one-bit converter** switches a pin on for the input's share of 4,096 of the 28 MHz ticks;
  a resistor and capacitor smooth it. It has no sample rate.
- The **internal speaker** plays the left and right values added, through one more converter; NextReg
  `$08` bit 4 switches it on, and with NextReg `$06` bit 6 it plays only the beeper, which then leaves the
  jack and HDMI.
- **HDMI** sends the whole sum as 16-bit words, 48,000 a second, taking the sum's value at each sample
  without averaging; NextReg `$09` bit 2 silences it.
- **Klive** mixes in the same units, counts the DACs and the Pi from their resting levels, filters the
  beeper's constant level away and multiplies by **12.8**, so its range matches the jack's: what the jack
  plays cleanly, Klive plays cleanly.
